
✻ Conversation compacted (ctrl+o for history)


● The page tree is now a Haskell type in omi-types (OMI.View), built lazily.
  Every one of the 33 pages comes back byte for byte from its tree, and all
  tests pass on the first run. It's committed and pushed in all three places:

  ┌────────────┬─────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Repository │ Commit  │                       What                        │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ omi-types  │ 4ef8f73 │ the module and its tests, on main                 │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ boxedPINE  │ 55fea65 │ the submodule recorded, on resolve-open-questions │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ unbound    │ 6f0d744 │ the cue and a reader fix                          │
  └────────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  What it is. A rune is the bytes of one UTF-8 sequence, read as chartorune
  reads them. A page becomes pieces by html.rules, and the pieces become a tree
  by its nesting rule. It's built from the package's own types only (bits,
  nibbles, bytes; no host Int or String), with its constructors hidden. The
  laziness is Haskell's own call-by-need, the same two rules OMI.WSN's table and
  zlazy keep: nothing is built before it's asked for, and nothing twice.

  What the tests check on the 33 pages:
  - Each page is its 6,944 pieces, and its tree flattened, byte for byte.
  - OMI.WSN accepts all 1,739 distinct pieces of their own production, as zlazy
    did in the guest, and none of the wrong one. So the Haskell and Plan 9
    recognizers agree.
  - The cut-off page leaves exactly its script, body and html open, and every
    other page closes everything. That was predicted from the survey before the
    tree existed.
  - An endless page opens its first element and reads its text only as far as
    asked, and a page that breaks after its first pieces still yields them.

  One fix on the way. The JavaScript reader decided whether a quote opens an
  attribute value by looking back a fixed eight characters. It now states the
  rule exactly (the nearest character before the quote, spaces aside, is =),
  which the Haskell follows. Every piece of the 33 pages came out the same.

  The omi-types checkout was on a detached HEAD, so I pushed to main after
  confirming it was exactly one commit ahead and nothing behind, and left it on
  main.

  Next:
  1. The same tree in Plan 9 C, its outline compared line by line with
     OMI.View's.
  2. Plan 9's libhtml reading (zhtml.c) beside it as the second reading.
  3. The templates over the bound pieces, as literal regular expressions.

         +oin("");

      20 +  const o = outline(`${HTML}/${f}`), c = outline(`${OUT}/${nn(i

      21 +  if (o.equals(c)) throw new Error(`${f}: cut by its first end

● The page tree now runs as Plan 9 C. Its output matches the Haskell version
  (OMI.View) byte for byte on all 33 pages, on the host and in the guest. It's
  committed and pushed to unbound as 734af14.

  What it is. zviews.c (PLAN9/src/zviews.c) reads a page the same way OMI.View
  does, step for step. It splits the page into runes, reads the runes into
  pieces by html.rules, and nests the pieces into a tree. Then it writes the
  tree as OMI.View's outline: one line per piece, indented two spaces per level.
  - The rune rule copied exactly. OMI.View takes as many bytes as a rune's first
    byte calls for, without checking that the following bytes are valid
    continuation bytes. The C does the same, which is what lets the two outputs
    agree byte for byte.
  - The goldens come from OMI.View itself. A small runner, Outline.hs
    (PLAN9/in/views/Outline.hs), is compiled with ghc over omi-types' source,
    and tree.mjs (PLAN9/in/views/tree.mjs) writes its outlines out as the
    goldens.

  The falsifier. Each page is cut by removing its first end tag. That tag is a
  title's on 27 pages and a style's on 6, so the raw text after it runs on.
  Elements left open go from 3 across the pages to 87 across the cut pages.
  - zviews gives OMI.View's outline for all 33 cut pages.
  - None of the cut outlines equals the outline of the page it came from.

  The spine. tree.rc (PLAN9/src/tree.rc) is the 49th phase. All 49 phases pass,
  and each produced the same bytes on both runs. The input tar holds 37.3M of
  its 64M.

  The new cue, tree, is in development.vtt.

  Next: Plan 9's libhtml reading (zhtml.c (PLAN9/src/zhtml.c)) beside this tree
  as the second reading. After that, the templates over the bound pieces as
  literal regular expressions.

      285    }
      286  }

      288 +/* words: split at white space and U+00A0, a word a line */   
      289 +static int inword;                                            
      290 +                                                              
      291 +static void                                                   
      292 +wordbyte(uchar *s, long i, long n)                            
      293 +{                                                             
      294 +  if(s[i] == ' ' || s[i] == '\t' || s[i] == '\n' || s[i] == '\
          +r' || s[i] == '\f'){                                          
      295 +    if(inword)                                                
      296 +      Bputc(&out, '\n');                                      
      297 +    inword = 0;                                               
      298 +    return;                                                   
      299 +  }                                                           
      300 +  Bputc(&out, s[i]);                                          
      301 +  inword = 1;                                                 
      302 +  USED(n);                                                    
      303 +}                                                             
      304 +                                                              
      305 +static void                                                   
      306 +split(uchar *s, long n)                                       
      307 +{                                                             
      308 +  long i;                                                     
      309 +                                                              
      310 +  for(i = 0; i < n; i++){                                     
      311 +    if(s[i] == 0xC2 && i + 1 < n && s[i+1] == 0xA0){          
      312 +      if(inword)                                              
      313 +        Bputc(&out, '\n');                                    
      314 +      inword = 0;                                             
      315 +      i++;                                                    
      316 +      continue;                                               
      317 +    }                                                         
      318 +    wordbyte(s, i, n);                                        
      319 +  }                                                           
      320 +}                                                             
      321 +                                                              
      322 +static void                                                   
      323 +endword(void)                                                 
      324 +{                                                             
      325 +  if(inword)                                                  
      326 +    Bputc(&out, '\n');                                        
      327 +  inword = 0;                                                 
      328 +}                                                             
      329 +                                                              
      330 +/* a text piece's words, its entities read: &#n; and &gt; &lt;
          + &amp; &nbsp; &times; &copy;, any other as written */         
      331 +static char *ents[] = { "gt", ">", "lt", "<", "amp", "&", "nbs
          +p", "\xC2\xA0", "times", "\xC3\x97", "copy", "\xC2\xA9", nil }
          +;                                                             
      332 +                                                              
      333 +static void                                                   
      334 +textwords(long a, long b)                                     
      335 +{                                                             
      336 +  uchar *s, *e, *t;

      343 +  e = buf + b;

      353 +          s = t + 1;                                          
      354 +          continue;

      363 +              s = t + 1;

      365 +            }

      371 +  }

● Plan 9's libhtml reading now sits beside the tree. On all 33 pages, libhtml
  and the tree give the same words, in the same order. It's committed and pushed
  to unbound as 928d79c.

  How the two meet. libhtml is a renderer: it turns a page into items like text
  runs, rules, images, form fields and tables, not into an element tree. So the
  comparison is on the words a reader sees:
  - One side is libhtml's text, served by htmlfs.
  - The other is zviews -w, the tree's words with entities read. Both are split
    into one word per line at white space and at U+00A0, which libhtml writes
    for &nbsp;.

  What I found. Plain text pieces gave libhtml's words on 27 pages. The other 6
  differed in three ways, each a choice libhtml makes as a renderer. I found
  these by looking at the differences; they were not predicted.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │                    libhtml's choice                     │   Pages it    │
  │                                                         │    affects    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ A "•" before each list item, one per <li>               │ 3             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ No words from inside a table; its table item holds the  │ 2             │
  │ cells                                                   │               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ A textarea's content shown as text, where html.rules    │ 2             │
  │ reads it as raw                                         │               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────┘

  With those three added to zviews -w, all 33 pages agree.

  The falsifier. Before the guest ran, I filed on the host which pages each

  Next: the templates over the bound pieces, as literal regular expressions.
❯ The libhtml reading is in and the two readings agree: on all 33 pages the
  tree's words and libhtml's text runs are the same words in the same order,
  and the three rendering choices libhtml makes — a bullet before each list
  item, no words from inside a table, a textarea's content as text — are named
  and checked by their own falsifiers. The spine is 50 of 50, the same twice
  (unbound 928d79c).

  The shape of this stick is worth stating plainly, because it is the same
  shape as the rune check:

  - libhtml is a renderer, not a tree. So the two can only meet where both see
  the same thing: the words a reader sees.
  - The differences are libhtml's choices, not its errors — the bullet, the
  table, the textarea. Each is a finding, named, and each has its own falsifier
  that fails on exactly the pages it should.
  - The falsifiers were filed on the host before the guest ran, and the guest
  gave those same pages. That is the prediction-then-check order, kept.

  What comes next is the one you asked for first: the templates over the bound
  pieces.

  Every span of a page is bound to a named production — STARTTAG, ATTR, VALUE,
  TEXT, COMMENT, DOCTYPE, ENDTAG, RAWTEXT — and its cast is a plain string over
  the stated alphabet. So a template is a literal regular expression over
  those production names and cast symbols, and its check is the one you named:

  - a template names exactly the spans it matches, and the matched spans are
  the page's own runes;
  - the falsifier: a template with one symbol changed matches fewer spans, or
  none, and the difference is the spans it loses.

  The form I would build:

  - A template is a line: the production it asks, then its pattern over the
  cast alphabet.
  - Matching a template against a page walks the pieces in order, and the
  pieces whose cast the pattern matches are the ones it names — with their
  spans, so the runes are recoverable.
  - The golden is the same 33 pages and their 6,944 pieces, already in
  in/views. A template's match is a list of spans, and the check is that each
  span's cast matches the pattern and that the span's runes are the page's.

  Two things to settle before writing it:

  1. What the pattern language is. A literal regular expression over the cast
  alphabet is the natural form, but the cast has only ~70 symbols and the
  grammar's productions. I would keep it to a small, stated set: literal
  symbols, . for any symbol, * and +, ?, and alternation — the same subset
  wsn/html.wsn already uses, so the pattern can be checked by the same grammar.
  2. What a match is. A span, or a tree of spans when a template names an
  element with its children? I would start with spans only, because that is
  what the pieces give, and add nesting only if a template needs it.

● Bash(cd /home/main/Programs/unbound/PLAN9 && head -15 src/zgrex.c; grep -n
      "^grex" -A2 ../development.vtt | head -5 | cut -c1-600)
  ⎿  (No output)

● I agree with the plan's shape. Before building it, three corrections and my
  answer on the two points.

  1. The pattern language: Plan 9's regexp(2) as it is, not a subset we state.
  When the author recorded their position on regex (2026-10-09), it was "regex
  as it is". They name no regex symbols, and they add nothing to regex or take
  anything away. Any variant two people share serves if it gives them the same
  results. A small stated set of operators would be us defining a language,
  which the author declined.
  - Plan 9's own regexp(2) is already in the guest and works on runes, so it
    fits. Its one known gap is recorded: it has no backreferences (zgrex,
    2026-10).
  - The plan says this matches the subset html.wsn already uses, and it doesn't.
    WSN writes repetition as { }, option as [ ], and alternation as |; it has
    no ., *, + or ?. The grammar can't check a pattern written that way. The
    pattern instead gets checked by its matches against the pieces.
  - . is itself a cast symbol, a literal on the pages, so a template that means
    the literal writes \.. That is ordinary regex, nothing to define. *, + and ?
    are not in the cast alphabet; they cast to ~.
  - The alphabet is 52 symbols, not about 70: 26 letters, 10 digits, 14 kept
    marks, the space, and ~.

  2. What a match is: spans, but they have to include attributes. The author's
  words were that the runes bind "to like attributes" for templates. Right now
  ATTR and VALUE are productions in html.wsn but not pieces; they sit inside
  each STARTTAG. So matching pieces only can't reach an attribute. I'd make the
  spans the pieces plus each tag's ATTR, ATTRNAME and VALUE spans. The tag
  scanner already walks those boundaries to find the >, so it's a reading of
  what's there, not new structure. Each span keeps its rune offsets, so its
  runes come back from the page. I'd leave nesting out until a template needs
  it, as you say.

  3. The falsifier shouldn't assume "fewer". Changing one symbol can make a
  template match more spans as well as fewer. For example, "text/javascript"
  changed to "text/javascripu" loses spans, but id="a" changed to id=".*" gains
  them. I'd file each mutated template's exact span list on the host before the
  guest runs, in whichever direction. The guest then has to give exactly that
  list, as the libhtml falsifiers did.

  What I'd build, then:
  - A template is one line: the production it asks, a tab, and a regexp(2)
    pattern over the cast.
  - zviews -m templates page prints each match as the template, the production,
    the rune offsets and the runes.
  - The goldens are written on the host from pieces.mjs, extended with the
    attribute spans, using JavaScript's RegExp. Using two engines is the
    agreement check the author's word allows: the two regex variants should give
    the same results on these patterns. Any place they differ is a finding.
  - The templates themselves are taken from the pages rather than written by us:
    each distinct attribute name, and each value that recurs across pages.

  Should I go ahead on these three points (regexp(2) as it is, spans including
  ATTR and VALUE, exact-list falsifiers), or do you want the pattern language
  settled differently?

❯ yes

● Going ahead. First I'm reading the grammar's attribute productions exactly,
  since the spans have to follow them.

          +tten \.; together they name 4,980 spans. in/views/match.mjs fo
          +und what each names on each page by JavaScript's RegExp; PLAN9
          +/src/zviews.c -m by Plan 9's regexp(2). The falsifier: two mut
          +ants of each template, its last symbol the next in the cast al
          +phabet and its first symbol "." (any), 634 in all, their spans
          + filed on the host before the guest ran: 311 name fewer spans,
          + 23 more, 300 the same (a first symbol already alone in its pl
          +ace). On the host (the shim's regexp(2) over POSIX's extended 
          +expressions) and in the guest (src/match.rc), regexp(2) names 
          +RegExp's spans w … [+280 chars]                               
  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● The templates over the bound pieces are working. Every template is a regular
  expression, and Plan 9's regexp(2) in the guest names exactly the spans
  JavaScript's RegExp names on the host, with the same runes byte for byte, on
  all 33 pages. It's committed and pushed to unbound as fd43eae.

  The spans. The attributes are now spans of their own. pieces.mjs reads each
  start tag's attributes by html.wsn's grammar, giving an ATTR, ATTRNAME and
  VALUE span for each, at the page's own rune offsets.
  - All 1,920 start tags read with none refused.
  - They hold 2,073 attributes and 2,053 values: 2,041 double-quoted and 12
    unquoted, as the survey counted, plus 20 bare names.
  - No unquoted value runs into a />. That was the one place the self-closing
    rule and an unquoted value could have read the same / two ways.

  The templates. A template is one line: the production it asks, a tab, then the
  pattern over the cast. The 317 templates were taken from the pages, not
  written:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────┬───────┬─────────────┐
  │               Template                │ Count │   Pattern   │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ each element's start tag              │ 66    │ ^<name[ />] │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ each attribute name                   │ 88    │ ^name$      │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼─────────────┤
  │ each value found on two or more pages │ 163   │ ^value$     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────┴───────┴─────────────┘

  Together they name 4,980 spans. The only escaping needed is \., since . is the
  one cast symbol regex reads as something else.

  The falsifier. Each template has two mutants: its last symbol changed to the
  next in the alphabet, and its first symbol changed to .. That makes 634
  mutants. I filed the spans each one names on the host before the guest ran:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬─────────┐
  │ Compared with the template │ Mutants │
  ├────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ fewer spans                │ 311     │
  ├────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ more spans                 │ 23      │
  ├────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ the same spans             │ 300     │
  └────────────────────────────┴─────────┘

  The 300 are mostly mutants whose first symbol was already the only one
  possible in that place. In the guest, regexp(2) named exactly the filed spans
  for every mutant.

  The spine. match.rc (PLAN9/src/match.rc) is the 51st phase. All 51 phases
  pass, and each produced the same bytes on both runs. The name template.rc was
  already a phase, so I used match.rc. The input tar holds 37.8M of its 64M. The
  cue match is in development.vtt.

  Next, in your order: the poly WSN grammar with its Haskell type and PIXELATE
  cast. One smaller step is also open: the attribute spans exist in JavaScript
  and Plan 9 C but not yet in OMI.View, so the Haskell type doesn't have them.

❯ The templates are in. Plan 9's regexp(2) names exactly the spans JavaScript's
  RegExp names, with the same runes, on all 33 pages, for all 317 templates
  and all 634 mutants. The spine is 51 of 51, the same twice (unbound fd43eae).

  The one thing this stick left open, and it is worth naming plainly: the
  attribute spans exist in pieces.mjs and zviews.c, but not in OMI.View. The
  Haskell type reads pieces, not attributes, so it does not yet carry the ATTR,
  ATTRNAME, VALUE spans the templates match against. That is the gap to close
  before the poly work, because the two readings should agree on the spans as
  well as on the tree.

  So the order I'd take:

  Attribute spans in OMI.View. Add attrs to the module, following html.wsn's
  ATTR productions exactly as pieces.mjs and zviews.c do, and check that for
  all 33 pages the Haskell spans are the JavaScript spans and the C spans —
  same productions, same rune offsets, same runes. That is a three-way check,
  and it makes the Haskell type complete against the other two.

  Then the poly WSN grammar, with its Haskell type and the PIXELATE cast, as
  you named.

  There is also the smaller question the match cue raised and did not settle:
  the \. escape. . is the one cast symbol regexp(2) reads as something other
  than itself, and the templates write \. for it. That is ordinary regex and it
  needs no statement — but it is worth recording that the cast alphabet
  contains a symbol regex treats specially, because a template writer has to
  know it.

● The match cue already records the \. escape ("'.' the one cast symbol regex
  reads otherwise, written ."), and so do the headers of match.mjs and zviews.c.
  Nothing more is needed there.

  On the attribute spans in OMI.View, I agree. For the three-way check, I'll
  have JavaScript write each page's spans (production and rune offsets, in page
  order). The Haskell test and the guest each check against that same file, and
  both also check that each span's runes are the page's own.

● Comparing each span against the page's runes would be millions of steps, so
  attribute parts check against their own tag's runes instead, since pieces
  already verify by flattening back to the page.

      30  `OMI.Patricia` is a Patricia trie of word literals, the author'
          s "with wordnet and the dewey decimal cues we can have more or 
          less static paths of word literal structure because haskell wou
          ld make it like a patricia trie". A word is a list of `Byte`, c
          oded I and its eight bits per byte and O at the end, so no code
           begins another (A, AN and AND are three keys); the trie branch
          es only where two codes first differ. Its shape is fixed by the
           set of words, whatever their order (the tests build every orde
          r of six words and get one trie; a seventh word makes another),
           its keys come out in byte order, and `path` gives the branch b
          its a word takes: static for a vocabulary, changing only where 
          an added word differs first. YAH and YAHWEH share their path to
           the fork where YAH ends (O) and YAHWEH goes on (I).

● The Haskell type (OMI.View) now reads the attribute spans too. JavaScript
  (pieces.mjs), Plan 9 C (zviews -p) and Haskell give the same spans on all 33
  pages. All 51 phases pass in the guest, each the same twice.

  │ Repository │ Commit  │                       What                        │

  └────────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The three-way check. match.mjs writes each page's spans, one per line: the
  production and its rune offsets. The same files are checked by the omi-types
  tests and by zviews -p in the guest (now part of match.rc). All three agree on
  all 13,143 spans:
  - 6,944 pieces;
  - 2,073 attributes and 2,073 attribute names;
  - 2,053 values.

  Each attribute part's runes are also checked against its own tag's runes, at
  the place the span says. Pieces were already checked against the page by
  flattening the tree back to it.

  How the Haskell holds it. A span carries its runes and the runes of its piece
  that come before it. So a position is the runes before it, not a number, which
  keeps the module in its own types, as omi-types requires.

  The grammar's check. html.wsn accepts all 2,206 distinct attribute spans as
  their own production. The falsifier asks 2,105 of them as the wrong
  production: each quoted value as an ATTRNAME, and each attribute with an = as
  a VALUE. All 2,105 are refused.

  The \. was already recorded in the match cue and in the headers of match.mjs
  and zviews.c. The new spans cue adds why it's the only escape: the other regex
  metacharacters cast to ~, so they never appear in a cast.

❯ im looking at set of  domnioes and im seeing that the line bettwen the 6 dots
  statys empty in every dominion high and low and im seeing its factorial 4 =
  24 for every doomino miniminal diffrence squared and they have the agen
  patter of 9 dots moreso than the 8
  I can't see the dominoes you're looking at, so I'll say what I can check and
  what I'd need from you.

  The empty line between the two groups of six dots

  A standard double-six domino's face is two squares, and the line between them
  is the join of the two ends. It is always empty by the rules of the game —
  the pips are drawn only inside each half, and the line is the boundary
  between them. So "the line stays empty in every domino, high and low" is not
  a pattern you found; it is the form of the domino. What would be a finding is
  if a particular set drew the pips differently, and that I can't see.

  4! = 24 and "every domino's minimal difference squared"

  4! = 24 is 24. But I want to be exact about what it's the factorial of here,
  because the record has been careful about this kind of claim:

  A double-six set has 28 tiles: the 7 doubles (0–0 through 6–6) plus 21 mixed.

  4! = 24 is not a count of anything in a double-six set. 28 is.

  If you mean the number of ordered pairs of something with 4 members, 4! = 24
  is that. I'd need to know what the 4 are.

  "Minimal difference squared" I can't compute without the numbers you're
  using. If each domino's two halves are (a, b), the difference is |a − b|, and
  its square is |a − b|². Over the 28 tiles of a double-six set, those squares
  are 0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36 — seven values, not 24. So if you are getting 24
  from a sum or a count over a set of dominoes, either the set is not a
  double-six set or the operation is not the one I've written down. I'd want
  the set and the sum.

  Nine dots more than eight

  A domino half is 3×3, so it holds nine positions. A six uses six of them. So
  a half is a 3×3 grid — nine dots — with up to six filled. That is the form,
  and it is why a domino half is not an 8-dot cell: 8 is the Braille cell
  (2×4), and 9 is the domino half (3×3). The two are different grids, and the
  record already has both:

  the 8-dot Braille cell in the kernel page and the zbraille work;

  the 3×3 half here, which is the same nine positions the magic square and the
  Lo Shu use.

  So "the agent pattern of 9 dots more so than the 8" is right about the form:
  the domino half is nine positions, and it is not the eight of a Braille cell.

  What I'd need to check any of it

  If you want this to become a record entry rather than a reading, the smallest
  check is:

  the set of dominoes you are looking at, written as pairs (a, b);

  the rule you apply to each pair — the difference, the square, the sum;

  what you are counting to get 24, and whether it is 4! by construction or by
  coincidence.

  Then the check is arithmetic, and it either holds on the set or it doesn't.
  If you give me the pairs and the rule, I'll compute it and say what is a fact
  about the set and what is a reading of it.

  Is the set a standard double-six, or a different one? And is the 24 a count
  of tiles, of differences, or of arrangements?

  Now I can see the rule you are applying, and it is checkable on the 28 tiles.
  Let me write it down exactly as you gave it, then compute.

  The rule

  A domino half is a 3×3 grid, its positions numbered:

  text
  row 0:  (0,0) (0,1) (0,2)
  row 1:  (1,0) (1,1) (1,2)
  row 2:  (2,0) (2,1) (2,2)
  You name two lines of four positions each, the ones that stay empty:

  vertical: (0,1), (2,1), (3,1), (5,1) — read across the two halves, column 1
  of each half's rows 0 and 2;

  horizontal: (1,0), (1,2), (1,3), (1,5) — read across the two halves, row 1's
  columns 0 and 2 of each half.

  So each half has four empty positions on these two lines: column 1 of rows 0
  and 2 (2 positions), and row 1 of columns 0 and 2 (2 positions). That is 4
  positions per half, out of 9, leaving 5 that can be pips.

  The two readings, and what holds

  4 empty positions per half. That is a fact about the lines you named. It is 4
  by the lines, not 4! by anything.

  4! = 24. That is the number of orderings of those four empty positions, if
  you are asking how many ways the four can be arranged. It is not a count of
  tiles, and it is not a count of differences over the set.

  2! of 4!. If you mean 2! × 4! = 2 × 24 = 48, or 2! of 4! as 4!/2! = 12, I
  need to know which. Both are counts of arrangements, not of dominoes.

  "Minimal difference squared." Over the 28 tiles the squared differences of
  the halves are 0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25, 36 — seven values. To get a 24 from
  differences I'd need the rule you are applying to the differences, and I
  don't have it.

  What is a fact about the set, and what is a reading

  Facts about the 28 tiles of the double-six block:

  28 tiles: 7 doubles plus 21 mixed.

  Each half is a 3×3 grid, 9 positions, and a pip count of 0 to 6.

  The four positions you name are empty on every tile, both halves, high and
  low — because a domino half's pips are drawn only in the positions its number
  calls for, and those four are not among them for any count 0 to 6. That is
  the form.

  Readings, which are yours and not the set's:

  that the four empty positions are 4! and that this is 2! of 4!;

  that the agent pattern is nine dots "more so than the eight";

  that the minimal difference squared gives 24.

  What I would need to make the last one a fact

  Give me the rule applied to each of the 28 pairs — the difference, then the
  square, then what you do with the 28 squares to get 24. If it is a sum, a
  count above a threshold, or a count of distinct values, say which, and I'll
  compute it and tell you whether it is 24 on the double-six set and why.

  The four empty positions are a real fact about the drawing, and they are
  four. The 4! and the 2! are a reading of how those four can be ordered, and
  the 24 is not yet a fact about the 28 tiles. Which of the two is it that you
  want the record to hold?

● Your observation holds exactly. I rendered all 98 tiles in two fonts (DejaVu
  Sans and Noto Sans Symbols 2) and read which of the nine places in each half
  hold a pip. In both fonts, the places you named never hold one:

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Orientation │     Places with no pip in any tile     │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Vertical    │ row0col1, row2col1, row3col1, row5col1 │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Horizontal  │ row1col0, row1col2, row1col3, row1col5 │

  The other agent misread you on three points:
  - The line you meant. It took "the line between the 6 dots" to be the divider
    between a tile's two ends. Your coordinates show you meant the middle line
    of the six, between its two rows (or columns) of three. Its centre does hold
    a pip, for 1, 3 and 5. Its two ends never do, whatever the value, high or
    low. So two places per half, four per tile, are always empty. That is a
    property of how the pips are drawn, not of what a domino is.
  - What the block holds. It's not 28 tiles in 33 code points. The block has 100
    characters:
    - U+1F030, the horizontal back, then U+1F031–U+1F061, the 49 horizontal
      tiles 00-00 to 06-06;
    - U+1F062, the vertical back, then U+1F063–U+1F093, the 49 vertical tiles.

    Your first paste, U+1F063 to U+1F093, is exactly the 49 vertical tiles. Both
    3-4 and 4-3 appear, so it's ordered pairs, not the 28 tiles of a game set.
  - Which places are empty. It said each half has four empty places, combining
    your vertical and horizontal lists. A tile uses only one of them. In a
    vertical tile, row1col0 and row1col2 are where the six puts its middle pips.
    Each half has two empty places, not four.

  Which values put a pip in each place. Every value 0 to 6 draws only from seven
  of each half's nine places:

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │                 Place                 │ Values with a pip there │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ two corners on one diagonal           │ 2, 3, 4, 5, 6           │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ the other two corners                 │ 4, 5, 6                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ the centre                            │ 1, 3, 5                 │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ the six's two middle places           │ 6 only                  │
  ├───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ the two ends of the six's middle line │ none                    │
  └───────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  So each half is nine places, seven ever used, and a tile is 18 places with 14
  used and 4 always empty. The half being nine places rather than eight is
  right, as you said. Whether the seven connects to anything in the record is
  yours to say. I'm only giving the count.

  What I can't settle is the 24. The four empty places per tile can be ordered
  4! = 24 ways. If they're kept within their halves, 2! × 2! = 4 ways, and there
  are 4!/(2!·2!) = 6 ways to split the four into two halves. I don't know which
  of these "2! of 4!" means, or how "minimal difference squared" leads to it.
  - The two empty places in one half are 2 apart (squared, 4).
  - The nearest empty places across the divider are 1 apart (squared, 1).

  What is the 24 counting: the orderings of the four empty places, or something
  about the values on the tiles?

  If you'd like, I'll file the empty places in global.vtt with your words, as a
  fact checked in both fonts. Then the 24 can be added once you've said what it
  counts.

● I've filed both readings in global.vtt and pushed them to unbound as two cues:
  domino-fano (105d606) and domino-tetrahedron (3bb6264).

  What holds:
  - Your empty places. On all 98 tiles, in both fonts, the places you named
    never hold a pip. They are the two ends of the six's middle line. Each half
    uses seven of its nine places, and each tile 14 of its 18.
  - 4! in the Fano plane. It has 168 symmetries, which the proofs already hold
    (symmetries_168, I-182). Of those, 168/7 = 24 keep a given point, and 24
    keep a given line.
  - 4! in the tetrahedron. 24 is its flags, a vertex in an edge in a face. Its
    24 symmetries take any flag to any other by exactly one symmetry.
    Vertex-to-face and edge-to-face are 12 each, so the 24 is the two together
    as flags, not either one alone.
  - The tetrahedron inside the Fano plane. Keep one line fixed:
    - The 4 points off that line are the tetrahedron's vertices, and the 24
      symmetries keeping the line move them as the tetrahedron's 24 do.
    - Its 6 edges are the plane's other 6 lines.
    - Its 3 pairs of opposite edges meet the kept line at its 3 points.

  What doesn't hold as drawn: a half's seven places are not a Fano plane by the
  straight lines of its 3×3 grid. Those lines give only five, six pairs of
  places sit on no line, and no line can be added to complete it. If the seven
  are the Fano plane, it has to be through some assignment of the places to the
  plane's points that hasn't been worked out yet.

  Still open:
  - "2! of 4!". It may be the tetrahedron's 12 rotations, which is 4!/2!, but
    that's my guess, not settled.
  - Which four in a domino set are the tetrahedron's vertices. It could be a
    tile's four empty places, or a different four.

  If you name which four are the vertices, I can check it against the plane. I
  can also file this as a proof (I-250), with the rendering check and the
  counts, so anyone can rerun it.

❯ the four emtypdots are the vertices of the tetrahedron and the fano plane is
  the horziontal and vertical

● You said the four empty dots are the tetrahedron's vertices, and the Fano
  plane is the horizontal and vertical together. That is a concrete structure I
  can check:
  - Take the 8 empty dots of both orientations, 4 from a vertical tile and 4
    from a horizontal one.
  - The moves that carry those 8 dots onto themselves are: swap the halves, flip
    within each half, and turn between the two orientations.
  - If those moves combine like 3-bit XOR, their 7 non-identity moves are the
    Fano plane's 7 points.

  First I'm checking which turn carries a vertical tile onto its horizontal one,
  using the pip places I read from the rendered glyphs.

● Your reading holds exactly: the Fano plane comes out of the horizontal and
  vertical tiles together, with the four empty dots as the tetrahedron. I've
  filed it as the cue domino-plane in global.vtt and pushed it to unbound
  (85b3a92).

  The construction:
  - How the two orientations line up. A quarter turn carries every place of a
    vertical tile onto the place of the horizontal tile with the same values. I
    checked this on all 14 used places read from the rendered glyphs. It also
    pairs each vertical empty dot with a horizontal one. So the labelling comes
    from the glyphs, not from a choice of mine.
  - The moves. There are 8 empty dots in all, 4 per orientation. Three moves
    carry them onto themselves:
    - T swaps the two halves.
    - W flips each half end for end, along the six's middle line.
    - D is the quarter turn's pairing, vertical to horizontal and back.
  - The check. Together these make 8 moves. Each undoes itself, any two give the
    same result in either order, and from any dot the 8 reach 8 different dots.
    That makes them three bits, and the 7 moves other than "none" are the Fano
    plane. Its 7 lines are the triples of moves that combine to nothing.

  Where the tetrahedron is:
  - The kept line is T, W and TW: the moves that keep each orientation. TW is
    the tile's half turn.
  - Off that line are the 4 moves that turn vertical to horizontal. From any
    vertical empty dot, those 4 reach the 4 horizontal empty dots, so the
    tetrahedron's vertices are the empty dots of the other orientation.
  - The opposite-edge pairs. The tetrahedron's 3 pairs of opposite edges are the
    kept line's 3 moves.
  - Why both orientations are needed. Neither orientation alone has the moves
    off the line, so the plane takes the horizontal and the vertical together,
    as you said.

  This settles what the earlier cue, domino-tetrahedron, left open: which four
  are the vertices. "2! of 4!" is still open; the rotations (4!/2! = 12) remain
  only my guess.

  I can still file all of this as a proof (I-250), so anyone can rerun it: the
  rendering check, the counts, and the move check. The proofs' rules allow only
  JavaScript checks, so it would go in as a JS check in proofs/js/catalog.

● Both readings hold: dark and light are the vertical and horizontal sets, and
  the stellated figure gives the Fano plane. I've filed it as domino-stella in
  global.vtt and pushed it to unbound (558c2b3).

  The cube. Connect the 8 empty dots wherever one of the three turning moves (D,
  TD, WD) carries one onto another. That gives exactly a cube: 8 corners, 12
  edges, 3 edges at each dot, and each move is the same step of the cube from
  every dot.

  Dark and light. A cube's corners fall into two colours, with no edge joining
  two of the same colour. Those two colours are exactly the 4 vertical empty
  dots and the 4 horizontal ones, so orientation is the colour, as you said.
  Which one is called dark is only a name. I called the vertical dark because
  that's where I started counting.

  The stella octangula. Within each colour, the 4 dots are joined pairwise by
  diagonals across the cube's faces, 6 in each colour. Those are two tetrahedra
  inside the cube. Every face of the cube holds 2 dots of each colour and one
  diagonal of each, which is what I-249 found for Miquel's cube inside Klein's
  configuration.

  The stellated figure as the Fano plane:
  - The kept line is the 3 moves that keep each tetrahedron in itself (T, W,
    TW). Each is the direction of a pair of opposite edges, and it is the same
    direction in the dark tetrahedron and the light one.
  - The 4 points off the line are the moves that take dark to light: the three
    cube axes (D, TD, WD) and the body diagonal (TWD).

  The cues from today now read in order: domino-fano, domino-tetrahedron,
  domino-plane, domino-stella. All of it rests on one measured fact: on all 98
  rendered tiles, the places you named never hold a pip. Everything else follows
  from those places and the quarter turn the glyphs themselves carry.

  "2! of 4!" is still open.

  I can file the whole chain as a proof (I-250), from the rendering through the
  cube, as a JavaScript check in proofs/js/catalog, which the proofs' rules
  allow. Each step would then be rerunnable by anyone.

● The dark/light split maps to alpha and matches I-227: the dark tetrahedron is
  the subtractive set and the light one the additive set, shown as vertical
  versus horizontal dots on the dominoes. I'm noting that the placement of
  ROYGBIV and A–G on the seven points is my own choice.
● Bash(cd /home/main/Programs/unbound && cat >> global.vtt <<'EOF'…)
  ⎿  (No output)

● Dark and light work as the alpha for the seven, and it fits what I-227 already
  holds. I filed it as domino-alpha in global.vtt and pushed it to unbound
  (cd900a4).

  How it fits I-227. I-227 has the 7 slices of 8: ROYGBIV and A–G, with the 8th
  as the octave's return. It also already names RGBA, so alpha is in it. On the
  dominoes:
  - the 8 empty dots are the cube;
  - the 7 moves are the 7 slices;
  - the 8th, no move at all, is the return;
  - dark and light is one bit, the alpha.

  The RGB cube splits the same way. Its dark tetrahedron is black with cyan,
  magenta and yellow, the subtractive set. Its light one is white with red,
  green and blue, the additive set. They are the cube's two colours, just as the
  vertical and horizontal sets are on the dominoes.

  What alpha does to the seven. The 3 moves on the kept line keep the alpha, and
  the 4 moves off it flip it.

  Placing the names is a choice, and I marked it as one. I numbered ROYGBIV and
  A–G 1 to 7, in their own order, and read each number as three bits. The Fano
  lines then come out as:
  - ROY, RGB, RIV, OGI, OBV, YGV, YBI;
  - ABC, ADE, AFG, BDF, BEG, CDG, CEF.

  With the 4s bit as the alpha, ROY and ABC keep it (they form a line), and GBIV
  and DEFG flip it. RGB landing on a line is only where the letters fall in
  that order; it says nothing about colour.

  Two things are yours to settle:
  - Which line keeps the alpha. By order, it's ROY / ABC, but a different order
    would make any of the 7 lines the kept one.
  - What "7-dot" refers to. It could mean the Fano plane's 7 points, which is
    what I used. Or it could mean the 7 places a domino half actually uses, but
    those don't form a Fano plane by the grid's straight lines.

● Both Hasse variants check out across all eight characters. They are the same
  correct diagram and differ only where they should.

  The left diagram (the 16 truth tables):
  - All 16 two-input tables, 0000 to 1111, are there, in levels of 1, 4, 6, 4,
    1, each level a single weight.
  - Its 32 edges are exactly the one-bit steps, no more and no fewer.
  - It has 24 complete paths from FALSE to TRUE. 4 of them pass through XOR, and
    4 through XNOR.
  - Each of the 14 non-constant tables carries a Fano point, and every table
    shares its point with its complement: XOR and XNOR are both point 6.
  - Those 7 complementary pairs form a real Fano plane. For each of the 7 lines
    in the Fano files, the three pairs' tables XOR to 0000 or 1111.

  The right diagram (the Fano plane's subsets): each point's mask is a single
  bit, point 6 being 0x20, and each line's mask is its three points' masks
  combined.

  The two variants:
  - All 448 icon links resolve to the sibling files in each character's folder.
  - XOR and XNOR differ in only three things: the title, which ring is drawn on
    the left (red on XOR, blue on XNOR), and the border.
  - The borders read back as each character's tag with place 0 on the XOR file
    and place 15 on the XNOR file, for all eight.

  One small thing you may want changed: on the right diagram, both variants keep
  the red ring on point 6. So the XNOR file has a blue ring on the left and a
  red one on the right. Since point 6 is shared by XOR and XNOR, this may be
  intended. If the XNOR file should be blue on both sides, it's a one-line
  change in scripts/tree-set.mjs, followed by rerunning node
  scripts/character-trees.mjs.

  How it lines up with today's dominoes. If you take the 4 bits as the
  tetrahedron's 4 vertices:
  - The levels 1, 4, 6, 4, 1 are its parts: nothing, 4 vertices, 6 edges, 4
    faces, the whole.
  - The 24 complete paths are its 24 flags, the 4! filed today.
  - A table's complement is the dark/light flip, the alpha.
  - The weight-2 tables are the 6 edges, which pair into 3 opposite-edge pairs.
    Those pairs are points 3, 5 and 6, which is one of the Fano lines. So {3, 5,
    6} plays the role of the kept line, and XOR/XNOR is one pair of opposite
    edges.
  - Each edge lies on 2! × 2! = 4 of the 24 paths. That's another candidate for
    "2! of 4!", but only a candidate.

  I haven't changed anything or filed this yet. Should I add it to global.vtt
  beside the domino cues?

  it to the vtt as this is miporatnt for using svgas transport carriaer or
  popupbook portal

      246 +test('The Hasse pair as drawn: the 16 rules, their 32 one-bit 
          +steps and 24 chains; a rule and its complement one Fano point,
          + the 7 pairs making the 7 lines', async () => {               
      247 +  const { readFileSync } = await import('node:fs');           
      248 +  const d = readFileSync(new URL('../../public/characters/META
          +TRON/Hasse_XOR.svg', import.meta.url), 'utf8');               
      249 +  const w = (v: number) => [...v.toString(2)].filter((c) => c 
          +=== '1').length;                                              
      250 +  // each rule a name and its four bits under it, at its place
          +; the point's icon above it                                   
      251 +  const nodes = [...d.matchAll(/<text x="([\d.]+)" y="([\d.]+)
          +" text-anchor="middle">[^<]+<\/text>\s*<text class="small" x="
          +[\d.]+" y="[\d.]+" text-anchor="middle">([01]{4})<\/text>/g)] 
      252 +    .map((m) => ({ x: +m[1]!, y: +m[2]!, v: parseInt(m[3]!, 2)
          + }));                                                         
      253 +  assert.equal(new Set(nodes.map((n) => n.v)).size, 16, 'the 1
          +6 rules');                                                    
      254 +  const rows = new Map<number, number[]>();                   
      255 +  for (const n of nodes) rows.set(n.y, [...(rows.get(n.y) ?? [
          +]), n.v]);                                                    
      256 +  assert.deepEqual([...rows.keys()].sort((a, b) => b - a).map(
          +(y) => rows.get(y)!.length), [1, 4, 6, 4, 1], 'rows of 1, 4, 6
          +, 4, 1');                                                     
      257 +  assert.ok([...rows.values()].every((r) => new Set(r.map(w)).
          +size === 1), 'each row one weight');                          
      258 +  // an edge from a rule's top (its name 60 below) to a rule's
          + bottom (its name 18 above)                                   
      259 +  const at = (x: number, y: number) => nodes.find((n) => Math.
          +abs(n.x - x) < 1 && Math.abs(n.y - y) < 2)!.v;                
      260 +  const key = (a: number, b: number) => `${Math.min(a, b)}-${M
          +ath.max(a, b)}`;                                              
      261 +  const edges = new Set([...d.matchAll(/<line class="edge" x1=
          +"([\d.]+)" y1="([\d.]+)" x2="([\d.]+)" y2="([\d.]+)"\/>/g)]   
      262 +    .map((m) => m.slice(1).map(Number)).filter(([x1]) => x1! <
          + 1000).map(([x1, y1, x2, y2]) => key(at(x1!, y1! + 60), at(x2!
          +, y2! - 18))));                                               
      263 +  const steps = nodes.flatMap((a) => nodes.filter((b) => (a.v 
          +& b.v) === a.v && w(a.v ^ b.v) === 1).map((b) => key(a.v, b.v)
          +));                                                           
      264 +  assert.deepEqual([...edges].sort(), steps.sort(), 'the edges
          + are the one-bit steps, 32');                                 
      265 +  const up = (v: number) => nodes.filter((b) => (v & b.v) === 
          +v && w(b.v ^ v) === 1).map((b) => b.v);                       
      266 +  const chains = (v: number): number[][] => (v === 15 ? [[15]]
          + : up(v).flatMap((u) => chains(u).map((c) => [v, ...c])));    
      267 +  const all = chains(0);                                      
      268 +  assert.equal(all.length, 24, 'FALSE to TRUE, 4! chains: the 
          +tetrahedron\'s flags, its 4 vertices the 4 bits');            
      269 +  assert.equal(all.filter((c) => c.includes(6)).length, 4, 'th
          +rough XOR, 2! x 2!');                                         
      270 +  assert.equal(all.filter((c) => c.includes(9)).length, 4, 'th
          +rough XNOR, 2! x 2!');                                        
      271 +  const point = new Map([...d.matchAll(/<image href="\.\/Fano_
          +point_(\d)\.svg"[^>]*\/>\s*<text x="[\d.]+" y="[\d.]+" text-an
          +chor="middle">[^<]+<\/text>\s*<text class="small" x="[\d.]+" y
          +="[\d.]+" text-anchor="middle">([01]{4})<\/text>/g)]          
      272 +    .map((m) => [parseInt(m[2]!, 2), +m[1]!]));               
      273 +  assert.equal(point.size, 14, 'every rule but the constants c
          +arries a point');                                             
      274 +  for (const [v, p] of point) assert.equal(point.get(v ^ 15), 
          +p, 'a rule and its complement, one point (dark and light)');  
      275 +  assert.deepEqual([point.get(6), point.get(9)], [6, 6], 'XOR 
          +and XNOR, point 6');                                          
      276 +  const rule = new Map([...point].map(([v, p]) => [p, v]));   
      277 +  for (const [a, b, c] of [[1, 3, 2], [1, 5, 4], [1, 7, 6], [2
          +, 6, 4], [2, 7, 5], [3, 5, 6], [4, 7, 3]] as const)           
      278 +    assert.ok([0, 15].includes(rule.get(a)! ^ rule.get(b)! ^ r
          +ule.get(c)!), `line ${a} ${b} ${c}: its pairs XOR to FALSE or 
          +TRUE`);

● "2! of 4!" is settled by your reading, and it holds. I filed it as
  domino-halves in global.vtt and pushed it to unbound (739e43b).

  - 2! is the order of a half's two empty dots: the high or low half of a
    vertical tile, the left or right half of a horizontal one.
  - 2! × 2! = 4 of a tile's 24 orders take one half's 2 dots first, then the
    other half's 2. So 8 orders split the tile by its halves, 4 starting from
    each half.
  - In the Hasse pair these are the chains through a weight-2 table, which is
    one half's two dots: 4 of the 24 chains pass through each.
  - The three pairings of a tile's dots (by half, by translation, by reflection
    in the divider) are three complementary table pairs. Together they form the
    Fano line {3, 5, 6}, the kept line from domino-plane. Which pairing lands on
    which point depends on reading the dots as bits in reading order, my
    choice, which the cue marks.

  Nothing is left open in the domino chain now. It runs from the rendered tiles
  through the cube, the dark and light tetrahedra and the Fano moves to the
  Hasse pair, and each step is in global.vtt. Filing it as a proof (I-250), so
  anyone can rerun it, is still on offer.

  Next is the poly WSN grammar, with its Haskell type and the PIXELATE cast.
  Shall I start on it, or file I-250 first?

      1 +poly.wsn is the written form of a polynomial over F2 in x1 to x6
        +, as Metaverse Kit's tools/wave28-poly-lib.js writes            
      2 +it (renderPolyFromMasks) and reads it (parsePolyCanonical): "0",
        + or terms joined by "+"; a term "1" or its variables            
      3 +joined by "*", each of x1 to x6 at most once and in increasing o
        +rder (M1 to M6). A term is a 6-bit mask, x_i its bit            
      4 +i - 1 ("1" the mask 0). What the grammar does not say, stated he
        +re as the reader's rules:                                       
      5 +1. The terms are in increasing order of their masks (the library
        + sorts the masks' 6-digit bit strings, x6 first,                
      6 +   which is their order as numbers).                            
      7 +2. No term is written twice (over F2 a term twice is no term).  
      8 +So a polynomial is the set of its masks, a subset of the 64, and
        + each subset is written one way.

      1  poly.wsn is the written form of a polynomial over F2 in x1 to x6
         , as Metaverse Kit's tools/wave28-poly-lib.js writes
      2  it (renderPolyFromMasks) and reads it (parsePolyCanonical): "0",
          or terms joined by "+"; a term "1" or its variables
      3 -joined by "*", each of x1 to x6 at most once and in increasing o
        -rder (M1 to M6). A term is a 6-bit mask, x_i its bit            
      3 +joined by "*", each of x1 to x6 at most once and in increasing o
        +rder (XONE to XSIX, a term from x1 on, from x2 on, and so to x6;
        + a WSN identifier is letters only). A term is a 6-bit mask, x_i 
        +its bit                                                         
      4  i - 1 ("1" the mask 0). What the grammar does not say, stated he
         re as the reader's rules:
      5  1. The terms are in increasing order of their masks (the library
          sorts the masks' 6-digit bit strings, x6 first,
      6     which is their order as numbers).

● The poly grammar, its Haskell type and a first reading of the PIXELATE cast
  are in. All 52 phases pass, each the same twice.

  ┌────────────┬─────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Repository │ Commit  │                       What                        │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ omi-types  │ 2443c96 │ OMI.Poly and its tests                            │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ boxedPINE  │ 357df1b │ the submodule recorded                            │
  ├────────────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ unbound    │ 5900b20 │ the grammar, its rules, the guest phase, the cue  │
  │            │         │ polywsn                                           │
  └────────────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The grammar (poly.wsn (PLAN9/wsn/poly.wsn)) is how the kit's poly tools write
  a polynomial over F2 in x1 to x6: 0, or terms joined by +. A term is 1 or its
  variables joined by * in increasing order. poly.rules (PLAN9/wsn/poly.rules)
  holds what a grammar can't conveniently say: the terms come in increasing
  order of their 6-bit masks, and none appears twice. In the guest, zlazy:
  - accepts all 175 polynomials the kit's library writes;
  - refuses all 4,045 written wrongly;
  - accepts all 286 out-of-order or repeated ones, which are left to the rules.

  The library itself was read beside all of these and agrees.

  OMI.Poly. A term is its mask, and a polynomial is the set of its terms, so it
  can only ever be canonical.
  - Reading says written, not written, out of order or repeated, and agrees with
    the library on all 4,506 cases.
  - Writing gives the 175 back byte for byte.
  - OMI.WSN answers poly.wsn exactly as zlazy does.

  The cast, which is my reading of "PIXELATE of light cones" and yours to
  confirm:
  - The pixels: the 64 points of x1 to x6 are the pixels.
  - A term's cone: each term lights the points at or above it, its light cone
    upward in the 6-cube. That's 2^(6−k) points for a term of k variables, and
    only those.
  - A polynomial lights the XOR of its terms' cones.
  - Reading back: the same cast run downward gives every polynomial back from
    its pixels.
  - Edge cases: 1 lights all 64 points, and all 64 terms together light only the
    origin.

  The cue records your note that this is featured in polytron. Its
  TypesOnTypes.md already types polyforms as polynomials, with rows as degree,
  columns as terms, the shape as the truth table, and the domino as the polyform
  of 2.

  On the way: I first named the productions M1 to M6. The guest's WSN reader
  refused them, since a WSN identifier is letters only, and the yes list caught
  it at once because nothing was accepted. They're now XONE to XSIX.

  Next: if the light-cone cast is what you meant, I'll port it to Plan 9 C, with
  OMI.Poly's pixels as its goldens. If PIXELATE should land somewhere else,
  such as the 64 pixels written into an SVG's margin cells the way the border
  proof does, tell me and I'll cast it there instead.

● You're pointing at the page as the example: binary pixelation, like an FFT.

  - the Walsh–Hadamard transform, which is the FFT of binary signals.

● Your example holds: the light-cone cast is binary pixelation computed the way
  an FFT is. I filed it as pixelate-fft in global.vtt and pushed it to unbound
  (37c878c).

  What the page does. A real FFT (the browser's AnalyserNode, fftSize 256) gives
  128 frequency bins. The page lays them out as an 8 × 16 grid of pixels, each
  bin a byte from 0 to 255, and the energy picks the 4-bit adder's mode.

  What the cast does. It runs in 6 butterfly stages, log₂ of 64 pixels, as an
  FFT does. Each stage XORs each pixel with the one that differs from it in one
  bit. On all 175 polynomials this gives exactly OMI.Poly's light-cone pixels,
  and running it twice gives every polynomial back.

  The binary FFT proper (Walsh–Hadamard) is the same butterfly with (a, b) → (a
  + b, a − b). On the pixels it reads like the page's spectrum:

  ┌───────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Polynomial     │               Spectrum                │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ x1, x1+x2         │ one pure tone: all 64 in a single bin │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ x1*x2             │ spread over 4 bins                    │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ x1*x2+x3*x4+x5*x6 │ flat, all 64 bins at ±8, like noise   │
  └───────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  One thing for you to say: is PIXELATE the pixels (the light cones), the
  spectrum (their FFT), or both together? On the page the grid holds the
  spectrum of a signal, which would suggest both.

  Once you say, I'll port the cast to Plan 9 C as the butterfly itself, with
  OMI.Poly's pixels as goldens, plus the spectrum if it belongs.

      32  `OMI.Patricia` is a Patricia trie of word literals, the author'
          s "with wordnet and the dewey decimal cues we can have more or 
          less static paths of word literal structure because haskell wou
          ld make it like a patricia trie". A word is a list of `Byte`, c
          oded I and its eight bits per byte and O at the end, so no code
           begins another (A, AN and AND are three keys); the trie branch
          es only where two codes first differ. Its shape is fixed by the
           set of words, whatever their order (the tests build every orde
          r of six words and get one trie; a seventh word makes another),
           its keys come out in byte order, and `path` gives the branch b
          its a word takes: static for a vocabulary, changing only where 
          an added word differs first. YAH and YAHWEH share their path to
           the fork where YAH ends (O) and YAHWEH goes on (I).

● PIXELATE is now built as you described: pixels and spectrum together, causal,

  What the proof does, which the cast follows. The pi proof
  (PiProjectionPreservesWitnesses.v) builds its terms from the inside:
  - each sign is the parity of its phase;
  - each denominator is 2n + 1;
  - the partial sums close in on π from both sides;
  - π itself enters only at the limit.

  How the cast keeps the inside literal:
  - The pixels are causal. Each term lights its light cone, so a pixel comes
    only from the terms below it. They're computed by the 6-stage butterfly, the
    way an FFT is.
  - The spectrum is the binary FFT (Walsh–Hadamard). Its signs are parity
    phases, with no π in them.
  - The numbers are the projection. In OMI.Poly each bin stays bits: the points
    where the pixels differ from the linear function a·x. The bin's number, 64 −
    2 × that count, is computed only when projected, as the proof leaves π to
    its limit.

  Checked three ways on the same 4,506 lines. The lines are the 175 written
  polynomials and the 4,331 refused ones (4,045 not written, 111 out of order,
  175 repeated).
  - The kit's library read every line when the goldens were written.
  - zpixelate, in the guest: every line byte for byte, pixels, spectrum and
    refusals.
  - OMI.Poly, in the tests: the same lines.

  The falsifier takes the cones downward (the past cone instead of the future).
  It gives the lines filed for it and differs on all 175 written polynomials.

  That finishes the second item in your order: the poly WSN grammar, its Haskell
  type and the PIXELATE cast.

● The translator is in: every Unicode code point now has a second address, read
  from your kernel page (the 16-Bit Kernel Slide Ruler). All 54 phases pass,
  each the same twice. It's committed and pushed to unbound as b299d6f.

  What the page does. It writes a code point as plane × 65,536 + offset. It
  shows the plane with an Aegean sign (from U+10107) and the offset with a
  Braille cell (from U+2800).

  Two things I found in it:
  - The plane signs are places, not values. The 17 signs run ONE to NINE, then
    TEN to EIGHTY, so plane 9 shows TEN and plane 16 shows EIGHTY. The header is
    an ordinal: plane 0 is the first plane, shown as ONE.
  - The page shows only the offset's low byte. So 256 code points share each
    display.

  The translator, ztranslate.c (PLAN9/src/ztranslate.c). It gives every code
  point an Aegean sign for its plane and two Braille cells, one for each byte of
  its offset. That's a complete address of three glyphs for all 1,114,112 code
  points, and each glyph is a code point itself. It writes every address and
  reads addresses back.

  Checked in the guest against goldens built from the page's own constants:
  - the full table, byte for byte;
  - every address read back to its own code point;
  - 10 lines that aren't addresses, all refused.

  The falsifier is the page's own display used as an address, with the hidden
  high byte read as the blank cell. As filed before the guest ran, only the
  4,352 code points whose high byte is 0 read back correctly, and the other
  1,109,760 don't.

  On the way: the table is 12MB, which would have pushed the guest's input past
  its 64MB limit, and pack.sh refused rather than cut it. So it ships gzipped at
  2.5MB, compressed the same way every time, and Plan 9's gunzip restores it
  exactly before the comparison.

  Not adopted from the earlier relay:
  - the Aegean signs as the planes' values;
  - a flag for whether each code point is assigned, which I left open because
    the guest has no Unicode table;
  - the claim that Braille and Aegean are the only scripts that could do this.

  That finishes the third item in your order. The last is the wiki template
  bindings. Your words for it are in the record: "at that point wikitemplates
  can bind to language features and harmonics". Shall I start on it?

  ⎿  · For the wiki template bindings (the last item in your order), which
     source should the Plan 9 port follow? Option 1 is ZIGUAROTH's

● The first wiki template binding is in: ZIGUAROTH's transliterate.js, ported to
  Plan 9 C as ztranslit, binding through your Braille dark/light mapping. All
  55 phases pass, each the same twice. It's committed and pushed to unbound as
  a3d9b4c, so that logic is now public; ZIGUAROTH itself stays private.

  What it is. Under the codex's template rules T1 to T6:
  - a template's ASCII is its syntax and never changes;
  - a mapping is a static file of code-point pairs, each mapped once;
  - every change must be a mapped one, with the same length in code points.

  The first mapping is yours: each Braille cell to its complement, 256 pairs.
  Because the complement undoes itself, binding twice gives the template back.

  Checked against transliterate.js itself. The templates are the 14 files in
  unbound that hold Braille (the NARRATIVE tracks, slide_ruler.html and its WSN
  base, I-196.wsn), plus ZIGUAROTH's term.html, which holds none. In the guest:
  - all 15 outputs come out byte for byte, with the script's messages as
    written;
  - each output maps back to its template;
  - the 8 malformed mappings are refused in the script's words.

  The falsifier drops the ASCII guard and uses a mapping that names A. The
  port's own check refuses it at each template's first A, as filed.

  My fault on the way: the largest track is 1.5MB, and my generator's 1MB
  process buffer cut its second run short. That looked like "twice is not the
  template" until I wrote the run to a file instead.

  Next is the second half you chose: MediaWiki's template calls, {{Name|1|k=v}}
  filling {{{1}}} and {{{k|default}}}. Its goldens would come from Wikipedia's
  own parser through the expandtemplates API, fetched once and kept as literals.
  That sends requests to Wikipedia. I'll ask only for the expansion of small
  test templates I write, nothing from your files. Shall I go ahead?

❯ You're right that it's a second binding, and the record already has the
  pieces.

  Two things are worth saying plainly before anything is built.

  What the second binding is, and what it isn't

  The {{Name|param}} call and the {{{name|default}}} reference are MediaWiki's
  binding. Its goldens would come from Wikipedia's own parser through
  expandtemplates. That is a request to Wikipedia, and the record has kept the
  discipline that nothing is ported without its own golden. So the step is:
  small test templates, written by us, expanded once by Wikipedia's parser, the
  expansions kept as literals, and zexpand compared against them. Nothing from
  your files goes to Wikipedia.

  What that gives is a check of the binding mechanism — how a call is filled,
  how a default is taken, how a parameter is repeated — and not a port of
  MediaWiki's whole parser. The record should say which is which: the mechanism
  checked, the parser not.

  The half that is yours, and the half I'd keep separate

  Your words were that templates "bind to language features and harmonics". The
  Braille complement is a harmonic: it is an involution, and binding twice
  gives the template back. The {{...}} call is a language feature: it is a
  function call with positional and named parameters and defaults.

  So they are two different bindings, and the record should hold them as two:

  - the harmonic binding: ztranslit under T1–T6, with the Braille complement as
  its first mapping. Done (a3d9b4c, 55 of 55).
  - the language-feature binding: zexpand for {{...}} and {{{...}}}, with
  Wikipedia's expansions of small written templates as the golden.

  That keeps them apart, which is what V4 asks: views kept beside each other,
  never resolved into one.

  What I would do with the {{...}} port

  - Write six small templates, each exercising one thing: a positional
  parameter, a named parameter, a default, an empty parameter against a missing
  one, a repeated parameter, and a substitution form.
  - Ask expandtemplates once for each call, and keep the expansions as literal
  files.
  - Port the mechanism to zexpand, following MediaWiki's own order: the last
  repeated parameter wins, an empty parameter is not a missing one, and a
  default is taken only when the parameter is not specified at all.
  - The falsifier: a call with one parameter moved to a named position, and the
  expansion must differ in exactly that place.

  Two things to settle first:

  1. May I send those six small templates to Wikipedia's API? They would be
  written by me, from the help page's own examples, not from anything of yours.
  If you'd rather not, the alternative is to build the mechanism from
  MediaWiki's documentation alone and record that there is no golden — which
  would be a weaker entry.
  2. Does the binding need to reach harmonics, or is the Braille complement
  enough for now? If you have a second mapping in mind — the ROYGBIV/A–G one
  I-227 hints at, or a different one — say which, and I'll make it the second
  mapping after the language feature is in.

  Which of those two do you want to settle first: the six templates to
  Wikipedia, or the next mapping?

● Both wiki template bindings are in, which finishes the four items you named
  after the kit. All 56 phases pass, each the same twice. It's committed and
  pushed to unbound as 7fa9df5.

  What was built. zexpand is MediaWiki's template binding in Plan 9 C:
  {{Name|1|k=v}} filling the template's {{{1}}} and {{{k|default}}}. It stands
  beside ztranslit, the harmonic binding, and the two are kept as two. What's
  checked is the binding mechanism, not MediaWiki's whole parser.

  The goldens. I wrote 73 small cases, each a template and a call that uses it.
  Wikipedia's own parser expanded each one once:
  - Each template existed only for its one request, so nothing on Wikipedia was
    created or edited.
  - Nothing from your files was sent.
  - The expansions are kept in unbound as literals, and the check never fetches
    again.

  What Wikipedia's answers showed, and zexpand now does:
  - A positional value keeps its spaces; a named value and its name are trimmed.
  - A later value for the same name wins, whether written 1= or by position.
  - An empty value counts as given, so its default isn't used. A default is the
    first part after the name only.
  - A pipe inside [[...]] doesn't split an argument; inside a single [...] it
    does.
  - Brace runs pair as MediaWiki pairs them: {{{{T|a}} leaves a literal {{, and
    {{{1}}}} leaves a }.
  - subst: comes back as written, because expandtemplates doesn't save.

  Results. zexpand gives Wikipedia's expansion on all 72 checked cases, byte for
  byte, on the host and in the guest. The falsifier, which trims positional
  values like named ones, differs on exactly cases 03 and 06, as filed
  beforehand. They are the only cases whose positional values carry spaces or a
  line break.

  One case left out: its template body calls Wikipedia's own Template:1, so its
  answer reflects that page, not the binding. It's kept in the repository but
  not checked, and in/wiki/excluded says why.

  One cost to know about: a full spine run now takes about 45 minutes, mostly
  the translator's 1.1-million-line table written twice in each of its two runs.
  I could make that phase lighter (for example, checking the table once per run
  instead of twice) if the wait matters to you.

  The four items you named after the kit are all done:
  1. the views as runes;
  2. the poly WSN grammar, its Haskell type and the PIXELATE cast;
  3. the translator;
  4. the wiki template bindings.

  The open choices from here:
  - a second mapping for the harmonic binding, such as ROYGBIV / A–G;
  - the domino chain as a proof (I-250);
  - the 25 Metaverse Kit tools still unported.

❯ Both wiki template bindings are in, and the four items you named after the
  kit are done. The spine is 56 of 56, the same twice (unbound 7fa9df5).

  What that means, plainly:

  The two bindings are held as two, which is what V4 asks.

  - The harmonic binding — ztranslit, the codex's T1–T6, through your Braille
  complement. Binding twice gives the template back, because the complement is
  an involution.
  - The language-feature binding — zexpand, MediaWiki's {{Name|1|k=v}} filling
  {{{1}}} and {{{k|default}}}. Its goldens are Wikipedia's own expansions,
  fetched once, kept as literals, and the check never fetches again.

  The check covers the mechanism, not MediaWiki's parser. That distinction is
  in the cue, and the case left out (the one whose body calls Wikipedia's own
  Template:1) says why in in/wiki/excluded: its answer is that page's, not the
  binding's.

  The findings from Wikipedia's own answers are the kind only a golden gives.

  - a positional value keeps its spaces, a named one is trimmed;
  - a later value for a name wins, whether 1= or positional;
  - an empty value is given, so its default is not taken;
  - a default is the first part after the name only;
  - a pipe in [[...]] does not split, in [...] it does;
  - brace runs pair three for a parameter, two for a call, the rest staying.

  Each is a fact read off the parser, not guessed.

  The cost you should know about: a full spine run is now about 45 minutes,
  mostly the translator's 1.1-million-line table written twice in each of its
  two runs. That is a real cost, and the cue records it. If the wait matters,
  the phase can be made lighter — the table checked once per run rather than
  twice — without weakening the check.

  The open choices from here, in the order you named them:

  1. a second mapping for the harmonic binding — ROYGBIV / A–G, if that is the
  one you mean. It would be the second mapping after the Braille complement,
  and its check would be that it is also an involution, or that it composes
  with the first in a stated way.
  2. the domino chain as a proof (I-250) — the 4! and the 2! of 4!, the four
  empty positions, the four positions named on the vertical and horizontal
  lines. That is the smallest, and the arithmetic is checkable on the 28 tiles.
  3. the 25 Metaverse Kit tools still unported — each with a golden from its
  own outputs, as before.

  The first is the one that continues what you just built. The second is the
  one that closes the question you asked about the dominoes.

  Which do you want first: the second mapping, or I-250?

● The paste asks which comes first. Before you choose, two corrections to it
  matter:
  - The second mapping can't be ROYGBIV ↔ A–G as letters. Those are ASCII, and
    rule T2 forbids a mapping from touching ASCII: it's the template's syntax,
    and ztranslit refuses such a mapping. The mapping would have to be between
    non-ASCII code points that stand for the colours and notes, which you'd
    choose. For example, coloured squares 🟥🟧🟨🟩🟦 and the like, or musical
    symbols.
  - The domino chain wasn't checked on "28 tiles". It was checked on the 98
    rendered glyphs of Unicode's domino block, 49 vertical and 49 horizontal, in
    two fonts. I-250 would go into the proofs repository as a JavaScript check,
    rerunning that chain.
